                                                                ―20―
3. Business and Other Risks
Risk management system
  SHIONOGI takes appropriate management actions to create business opportunities and avoid or mitigate risks. At the same time, it
employs the Enterprise Risk Management (ERM) system, which oversees the entire Group’s business risks, including risks of crises such
as pandemics, natural disasters, terrorism, and cyberattacks, as an important mechanism for management strategy and management
foundations.
  In operating and managing ERM, both top-down and bottom-up approaches are employed. We classify risks that are inherent in
strategic decision making and may inhibit the implementation of strategies as “business strategy-related risks” and risks that may affect
the execution of business operations that support management objectives as “business execution-related risks.” By clarifying
responsibilities and ensuring transparency in the status of countermeasures, we conduct comprehensive risk management.
   Business strategy-related risks are discussed every quarter at the Corporate Executive Meeting, where risk lists are updated, risks that
need to be addressed are identified, and responsible units are appointed. Each responsible unit works with other units and relevant
organizations to formulate and implement risk response plans and reports the progress of the plans to the Corporate Executive Meeting.
In addition, for external trends that are changing rapidly and whose full scope and impact on the Company are not yet clear, we
continuously gather information and engage in timely discussions to identify emerging risks, thereby enabling us to identify risks that
need to be addressed and appoint responsible units.
  Business execution-related risks are managed by each organization, which serves as the risk owner and independently formulates and
implements plans to achieve its organizational goals. Each unit is responsible for monitoring the status of key risks under its purview
and, when necessary, escalating them to the Corporate Executive Meeting as “business strategy-related risks.”
   The Risk Management Secretariat serves as the secretariat for risk management operations at the Corporate Executive Meeting and is
responsible for administrative functions related to agenda setting, including compiling key risks and the status of responses, including
those at major subsidiaries in Japan and overseas, and identifying the most important risks among the business strategy-related risks that
have particularly significant impact on management. In addition, it researches external trends and environmental changes and provides
information to management in a timely manner, thereby enhancing and invigorating risk management discussions.
   These business strategy-related risks to be addressed by SHIONOGI and the status of responses thereto are reviewed and monitored
by the Corporate Executive Meeting and the Board of Directors twice a year, thereby ensuring the effectiveness of the risk management
process.

Crisis management/BCP
  Regarding crisis management, based on the crisis management rules, etc. and under a comprehensive management system, including
a business continuity plan, we promote management that focuses on respect for human life, consideration for local communities and
contributions, and control of corporate value damage. In the event of a crisis, we will deal with it and overcome it promptly.
  In addition, in order to enhance the effectiveness of risk management, we also focus on the development and operation of a system to
ensure proper business operations, based on the Basic Policy for the Construction and Operation of the Internal Control System.
  These activities are regularly reported to the Corporate Executive Meeting and the Board of Directors to receive advice for
improvement from Directors. We have thus established a system to supervise risk management activities.
  Matters and risks discussed here that are not historical fact reflect judgments made as of the end of the fiscal year ended March 31,
2026.




                                                                 ―21―
1. Business strategy-related risks
(1) Global growth centered on the infectious disease, QOL disease, and rare disease areas
  <Overview>
    The infectious disease area addresses serious diseases that threaten people’s lives and is of extremely high social significance. At
  the same time, it is characterized by relatively low market predictability, as revenue is susceptible to fluctuations depending on the
  prevalence of infectious diseases, public health policies, and requirements for appropriate use.
    Based on these characteristics, SHIONOGI has set “Protect people from the threat of infectious diseases” as one of its material
  issues and is committed to the continuous growth of the infectious disease area as a core global business. At the same time, against
  the backdrop of an increasingly super-aged society and changing lifestyles, we recognize the growing need to extend healthy life
  expectancy and improve quality of life (QOL) as a critical social issue, and we have therefore set “Contribute to a healthy and
  prosperous life” as one of our material issues. To achieve these material issues, we are accelerating our global business expansion.
     In the infectious disease area, including antimicrobial resistance (AMR), HIV, and acute respiratory infections, the QOL disease
  area, including depression, sleep disorders, and hearing loss, and the rare disease area, including fragile X syndrome, Pompe disease,
  and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, SHIONOGI is working to create healthcare solutions that comprehensively support disease
  prevention, diagnosis, treatment, and post-treatment management, with pharmaceuticals as the starting point, thereby aiming to build
  a more stable and sustainable revenue base that is less susceptible to infectious disease outbreaks.
    SHIONOGI has so far launched many products overseas mainly through partnerships with partner companies, in which it has
  received a part of the product sales as royalties. By making an additional investment in ViiV Healthcare and continuing to maintain
  good relationships with these partners, particularly in the HIV business, SHIONOGI is strengthening its global business development
  in the infectious disease area through business activities involving the development, regulatory submissions and approvals, marketing,
  and sales of SHIONOGI products in Europe, the U.S., and Asia, as well as activities to improve access to medical services in low- and
  middle-income countries.
    In addition to these activities, through the acquisitions of Torii Pharmaceutical and the JT Pharmaceutical Business, as well as the
  acquisition of the edaravone business for the treatment of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) from Tanabe Pharma, SHIONOGI is
  expanding its product portfolio, strengthening its R&D and sales capabilities, and building the business foundation for further global
  expansion, particularly in Europe and the U.S.
    However, if the growth strategies for these priority disease areas do not progress as originally planned, the expected future revenue
  growth may not be realized, which could have a significant impact on our performance.




                                                               ―22―
  <Major recognized risks>
  - Risk of declining sales in the HIV, AMR, and other areas within the infectious disease area due to changes in the competitive
    environment and market structure, resulting in reduced growth potential
  - Risk of delays or failures in the research and development of vaccines and new treatment drugs, resulting in delays to planned product
    launches in both the infectious disease and QOL disease areas
  - Risk of failure to realize the expected growth of the edaravone business due to changes in drug prices and the market conditions
   resulting from the implementation of pharmaceutical policies in the U.S.
  - Risk of post-merger integration (PMI) not proceeding as planned or of failing to realize the expected business value and synergies
   for M&A activities conducted in the QOL disease area
  - Risk that the development and commercialization of the HaaS (Healthcare as a Service) solutions may not proceed as planned,
   resulting in sales synergies in the infectious disease and QOL disease areas not being fully realized
  - Risk of missing sales opportunities in the infectious disease and QOL disease areas due to failure to establish sales infrastructure and
   secure personnel as planned in overseas markets, including the U.S.


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Steady implementation of PMI following the acquisitions of Torii Pharmaceutical, the JT Pharmaceutical Business, and the edaravone
    business, and maximization of business value in both the infectious disease and QOL disease areas
  - Strengthening the ongoing commitment to the HIV area through an additional investment in ViiV Healthcare
  - Negotiations with governments and regulatory authorities of various countries on stockpiling and expansion of the subscription-type
    reimbursement model
  - Prioritized allocation of resources to the development of vaccines and new treatment drugs in the infectious disease and QOL disease
    areas
  - Creating synergies by revising disease strategies across multiple disease areas
  - Strengthening internal and external collaboration for the development and expansion of HaaS solutions
  - Implementation of global development and application for approval, primarily in Europe and the U.S., and improvement of overseas
    production, distribution, and sales systems
  - Expanding sales of our main products in the Japanese market


(2) Expanding pipelines
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI has identified “Protect people from the threat of infectious diseases” and “Contribute to a healthy and prosperous life”
  as its material issues. To achieve both the resolution of these two social issues and sustainable growth, we are working to expand and
  enhance the quality of our research and development pipeline.
     In the infectious disease area, we have established the Shionogi Qpex lab. in the U.S. to strengthen our research capabilities, with
  a focus on treatment drugs for bacterial infections. At the same time, through collaborations with academia, startup companies, and
  U.S. government agencies, we are actively leveraging external expertise to accelerate drug discovery and improve its success rate.
  Through these efforts, we are working to strengthen our medium- to long-term research and development capabilities in the infectious
  disease area, including AMR.
     On the other hand, while we are seeing a clear path to business growth through M&A and in- and out-licensing activities carried
  out in recent years, we recognize the importance of maximizing synergies of existing research assets and talent, reviewing our pipelines
  and drug discovery programs from a holistic perspective, and evolving them into a robust and sustainable portfolio.
    In particular, strengthening our capabilities in the QOL disease and rare disease areas, maintaining and enhancing our competitive
  advantage in the HIV area, and strengthening our pipelines across the entire infectious disease area are the key priorities.
     In this environment with high uncertainty, in order to raise the success rate of drug discovery and build attractive pipelines that
  satisfy medical needs, in addition to the research and development technologies SHIONOGI has cultivated for infectious diseases and
  small molecule drugs, it is crucial to acquire new modalities, shift from a conventional business model relying heavily on revenue
  from pharmaceutical patents, and challenge ourselves to strengthen vaccines and sublingual immunotherapy and provide new
  healthcare services. Furthermore, as a foundation supporting these efforts, the continuous development of specialized talent and the
  accumulation of cross-disciplinary knowledge are essential. Accordingly, we are also working to strengthen our drug discovery and
  development capabilities through personnel rotation and other initiatives.




                                                                 ―23―
    We believe it is necessary to mitigate revenue fluctuations resulting from patent expiration by balancing the prescription drug
  business and other businesses. However, if these efforts do not progress sufficiently, or if the development of the talent needed to
  support them does not proceed as planned, our medium- to long-term growth potential may decline and it may become difficult to
  establish a competitive advantage in the future, which could have a significant impact on our business performance.


  <Major recognized risks>
  - Risk of failing to strategically integrate the pipelines in areas such as rare diseases and HIV, thereby failing to establish our unique
    medium- to long-term strengths and competitive advantages
  - Risk that external collaborations and efforts to strengthen research capabilities may not yield the expected results, resulting in a
    failure to accelerate research and development and improve its success rate
  - Risk of specialized research and development talent becoming concentrated in specific areas, resulting in insufficient development
    of the next generation of talent and stagnation in pipeline evolution and the creation of new value
  - Risk of failing to expand a competitive pipeline due to the obsolescence of existing technologies


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Review of strategies to optimize the overall pipeline and drug discovery programs in light of M&A and partnership deals
  - Ongoing investment in research and development to maintain a competitive advantage in the HIV area
  - Clarification of priority themes in the QOL disease area and optimization of R&D resource allocation through portfolio restructuring
  - Strengthening the infectious disease research framework, including Shionogi Qpex lab., and promoting collaboration with academia,
    startup companies, and government agencies
  - Development of talent with both specialized expertise and a cross-disciplinary perspective through personnel rotation across multiple
    research and development areas, including the vaccine business
  - Venturing into new modalities and technologies


(3) Human capital management
  <Overview>
    In order for SHIONOGI to transform its business model and achieve the growth targeted by the STS2030 Revision, each and every
  employee should constitute the “source of competitiveness” that leads the transformation, and SHIONOGI should be a group
  composed of such diverse human resources with strengths.
    In recent years, as the organization has expanded through M&A and business partnerships, the SHIONOGI Group has welcomed
  an increasingly diverse range of talent with different values, experiences, and expertise. Integrating this talent and enabling the
  organization to function as a cohesive whole has become an important management priority. In particular, as SHIONOGI continues to
  grow, it is becoming increasingly important to create an environment in which employees of Shionogi Pharma, which is scheduled for
  reintegration, as well as those joining the Group through Torii Pharmaceutical, the JT Pharmaceutical Business, and Tanabe Pharma,
  can perform to the best of their abilities with confidence while fostering a sense of unity as the SHIONOGI family and cultivating a
  new corporate culture.
    Furthermore, as we pursue business growth, it is essential to venture into areas beyond our previous experience and acquire new
  capabilities. This requires us to be an organization in which every employee embraces change positively and continues to take on
  challenges autonomously. Therefore, in addition to developing capabilities tailored to employees’ expertise and roles, we are also
  promoting flexible talent deployment and fostering a culture that encourages employees to take on challenges.
    Furthermore, recognizing that maintaining and improving employees’ physical and mental health, as well as ensuring the safety of
  workers at research and manufacturing sites, are fundamental to all these initiatives, we continue to promote the development of a
  work environment in which employees can work with peace of mind.
     However, if human capital management initiatives do not function effectively and the integration and development of talent, as well
  as the establishment of a culture that encourages employees to take on challenges, do not progress, the ability to execute our business
  strategy may decline, thereby undermining our future growth potential and competitive advantage.




                                                                 ―24―
  <Major recognized risks>
  - Risk of a decline in employee engagement and a loss of organizational cohesion as organizational structures and culture change due
    to M&A and other initiatives
  - Risk that the expertise and strengths of diverse talent may not be fully leveraged, leading to a decline in both individual and
    organizational performance
  - Risk of delays in business growth or medium- to long-term business stagnation due to a failure to systematically develop core talent
    who will lead SHIONOGI in the future
  - Risk of losing talented employees due to a lack of opportunities for growth or new challenges
  - Risk of establishing a corporate culture in which employees avoid taking on challenges for fear of failure, leading to a loss of
   adaptability to change


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Implementation of ongoing engagement surveys and promotion of improvement measures based on the results
  - Revitalization of internal communication and promotion of initiatives to foster mutual understanding with a focus on talent
    integration following M&A
  - Promotion of talent deployment and development measures aimed at deepening expertise while providing cross-disciplinary
   experience
  - Implementation of human resources systems, performance evaluation systems, and award programs that recognize employees’
    willingness to take on challenges and promote learning from them
  - Continuous strengthening of EHS Management to maintain and promote employee health and ensure worker safety


(4) Realizing reform by DX
  <Overview>
    Seeing recent technological innovations and the dynamic changes in the environment surrounding them as an opportunity,
  SHIONOGI declares in the STS2030 Revision its commitment to digital transformation through various activities as a big theme to
  accelerate decision making and realize new value creation based on data. As conventional business models are required to be
  transformed, it is essential to improve productivity using AI and IT. If the efforts to achieve this become stalled, it may have a
  significant impact not only on the performance of SHIONOGI but also on the improvement of its corporate value.


  <Major recognized risks>
  - Risk that the use of AI and IT may be limited to improving the efficiency of individual operations, rather than driving transformation
    of the entire business process or more sophisticated decision-making
  - Risk of stalled transformation due to a failure to sufficiently develop and secure talent and digital capabilities needed to drive DX
  - Risk that delays in establishing a globally integrated IT infrastructure and data utilization base may prevent collaboration and
    synergies across businesses and regions
  - Risk of failing to transform business processes into more competitive ones due to reliance on existing operations and traditional
    ways of working


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Building global IT infrastructure
  - Business model/operation reform through practicing AI drug discovery, market inventory prediction using AI, etc.
  - Developing a medical device program (SaMD) for diagnosis and treatment of diseases, and disease detection algorithms
  - Establishing a data utilization base that will improve work efficiency and realize new value creation
  - Implementing measures to develop digital core human resources




                                                                ―25―
(5) Responding to geopolitical and geoeconomic uncertainties
  <Overview>
    To achieve STS2030 Revision, SHIONOGI is accelerating its globalization efforts, with its business activities becoming
  increasingly global and complex through the advancement of global development, expansion into additional countries and regions,
  and the execution of in-licensing and out-licensing.
    At the same time, uncertainties surrounding business activities are increasing against the backdrop of changing global political and
  social conditions, including political instability and deteriorating economic conditions in various countries, social unrest, the outbreak
  and prolongation of regional conflicts, rising geopolitical tensions among countries and regions, tighter regulations on investment and
  imports and exports, changes in tariffs and trade policies, and stricter regulations on various transactions.
   SHIONOGI recognizes these growing geopolitical and geoeconomic uncertainties as significant business risk factors. By closely
  monitoring social conditions and policy developments, assessing their impact on our business, and promptly implementing appropriate
  measures, we strive to ensure the continuity of our global business operations and maintain stable access to medicines for patients.
    However, if unforeseen circumstances beyond our expectations arise, they could affect our business activities, including research
  and development, manufacturing, logistics, and sales, and have a significant impact on our performance.


  <Major recognized risks>
  - Risk that the continuation or expansion of our business activities may be constrained by rapid changes in political and economic
    conditions, social unrest, or the outbreak or escalation of conflicts in countries and regions around the world
  - Risk that tighter investment restrictions, economic sanctions, and other trade regulations resulting from heightened geopolitical and
   geoeconomic tensions may delay or hinder the implementation of business strategies, including in-licensing, out-licensing, and
   partnerships, or result in increased costs or supply constraints
  - Risk that disruptions to logistics and supply chains may hinder the stable supply of pharmaceuticals and patients’ access to them


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Continuous monitoring of political, economic, and social conditions and geopolitical trends in countries and regions around the
    world, and assessment of their impact on our business
  - Flexible review of business and investment plans in light of geopolitical risks, and maintenance and enhancement of a rapid decision-
    making framework when risks materialize
  - Ensuring a stable supply of pharmaceuticals and patients’ access to medicines through strengthening the resilience of global business
   operations across research and development, manufacturing, logistics, and sales




                                                                 ―26―
2. Business execution-related risks
(1) Quality and product safety
  <Overview>
   SHIONOGI manufactures products and outsources manufacturing under strict quality control in compliance with Good
  Manufacturing Practice (GMP) and the International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for
  Human Use (ICH) guidelines and other pharmaceutical-related laws and regulations. It has also been inspected by competent agencies,
  such as the Ministry of Health, Labour and Welfare in Japan, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) in the United States, and the
  European Medicines Agency (EMA), to obtain approval for production and sales. After a product is launched, efforts are made to
  gather safety information and take necessary measures for ensuring safety and proper use of pharmaceuticals. Since there are some
  areas of products and services for which global review standards have not yet been established, we strive to ensure the safety of the
  products and services themselves through individual discussions with competent authorities. It is also important that products and
  services, including pharmaceuticals, are used appropriately. We therefore provide information to our customers and work to raise their
  awareness about the relevant diseases. In the event of quality issues, such as quality defects or nonconforming lots, unforeseen adverse
  drug reactions (ADRs) or problems involving pharmaceuticals or product services, failure or delays in reporting ADRs, delays in
  responding to problems, or errors in the information provided by the Company, the following risks may arise.
  - Quality defect due to a discrepancy between approval documents and actual manufacturing conditions, shipment suspension, product
    recall, or suspension of business due to administrative action
  - A product recall due to lack of data integrity or a significant indication in an inspection by a competent agency
  - Suspension of sales or product recall
  - Lawsuits filed for compensation for health damage
  - Impact on business performance and reputation


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Establishment and promotion of the SHIONOGI Group Quality Policy
  - Promoting activities to foster a “quality culture”
  - Management and supervision activities through audits of manufacturing facilities, etc.
  - Establishing and strengthening systems to properly gather, analyze, evaluate, and report on safety-related information, such as side
    effects and other problems
  - Implementing quality and product safety training for all employees and management


(2) Global supply chain management
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI is committed to fulfilling its social responsibility through the stable supply of pharmaceuticals and is working to
  strengthen the resilience of its global supply chain to ensure the stable execution of its business activities.
     Due to natural disasters such as major earthquakes, storms, and floods, pandemics, the enactment of the Biosecure Act in the United
  States, and rising geopolitical tensions, the supply chain environment surrounding raw materials and products is becoming increasingly
  uncertain. Furthermore, against the backdrop of growing social demands regarding human rights and the environment, consideration
  for sustainability throughout the entire supply chain, including suppliers, is becoming increasingly important.
    In light of these environmental changes, SHIONOGI is working to mitigate the risk of supply disruptions by ensuring a stable
  supply of raw materials, selecting alternative suppliers, and maintaining appropriate inventory levels. At the same time, as excessive
  inventory buildup and a sharp rise in procurement costs may affect our business revenue and capital efficiency, we recognize that
  balancing a stable supply with cost control is a crucial management issue.
    SHIONOGI promotes global supply chain management that balances environmental and human rights considerations with business
  sustainability by comprehensively assessing risks across the supply chain and implementing measures that take into account both
  business and societal impacts.




                                                                ―27―
    However, if these initiatives do not function effectively and we fail to adapt to rapid changes in the external environment, the
  following risks may arise.
  - Suspension of plant operation
  - Difficulty in procuring raw materials and products
  - Disruption of wholesale distribution network and stagnation of information
  - Significant impact on stable supply of pharmaceuticals - Decline in profitability and capital efficiency due to excess inventory and
    increased costs
  - Damage to social standing and reputation


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Promotion of the optimization of inventory levels
  - Establishment of a system for the manufacture of active ingredients contained in some products in Japan
  - Diversifying raw material procurement sources to ensure a stable supply of products (selecting second vendors for raw materials
    with high geopolitical risk)
  - Establishing and periodically reviewing BCP items to be supplied on a priority basis
  - Conducting due diligence and audits on suppliers and requesting improvements


(3) IT security and information management
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI is committed to its social responsibility to support people’s lives and health through the research and development,
  manufacturing, and supply of pharmaceuticals, as well as the appropriate provision of pharmaceutical information. To ensure the stable
  and continuous execution of business activities, it is essential to properly manage confidential information, including personal
  information, and ensure the safe and stable operation of IT systems. As SHIONOGI conducts business globally, we utilize a wide
  range of IT systems and digital infrastructure, including those of our outsourcing partners. However, in recent years, cyberattacks
  targeting Japanese companies have become increasingly frequent and sophisticated, and attacks aimed at disrupting critical systems
  and compromising sensitive information have become increasingly prevalent in the healthcare and pharmaceutical industries.
    Pharmaceuticals are an essential infrastructure for society, and if the supply of products or the provision or collection of
  pharmaceutical or personal information stops, it may have a serious impact on patients and medical professionals. Therefore, we
  recognize that cybersecurity threats are not merely IT risks, but risks that directly affect business continuity and social trust. Based on
  this understanding of these risks, we are continuously working across the entire Group to establish and operate a robust and reliable
  IT security framework while strengthening information management and internal controls.
    However, if these initiatives do not function effectively, and negligent or intentional acts by employees or outsourcing partners, or
  cyberattacks or unauthorized access by malicious third parties, occur, the following risks may arise.
  - Difficulty in continuing business due to suspension of a critical or important system
  - Leakage of or damage to confidential information, including personal information
  - Emergence of legal and social liabilities, such as claims for damages and administrative responses, and increased costs associated
    with post-incident measures
  - Decline in social trust and reputation due to impact on the supply of pharmaceuticals or provision of information
  - Adverse impact on business performance resulting from the above


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Conducting education and awareness-raising to ensure that all employees of the Group are aware of the importance of information
   management and personal information and the need to comply with laws and regulations regarding the protection of personal
   information, as well as education and awareness-raising on the SHIONOGI Group Global Privacy Policy
  - Continuous strengthening of the information security management system, including security and internal fraud prevention
  - Enhancing the IT-BCP system to be prepared for a cyberattack, a large-scale disaster, or any other crisis
  - Improving IT infrastructure, strengthening information security infrastructure, and improving the infrastructure’s operational
   processes
  - Enhancing the Group-wide network structure based on a global security assessment conducted for the entire Group




                                                                 ―28―
(4) Compliance
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI regards compliance as the foundation that supports the survival and sustainable growth of the company and places it at
  the core of all its business activities. Furthermore, SHIONOGI recognizes that compliance not only refers to the observance of laws,
  rules, and regulations but also includes compliance with social norms and ethical behavior as a company and as members of society,
  and it promotes adherence to these principles throughout the Group. In recent years, with the globalization of business, the expansion
  of business areas, and the increasing complexity of organizational structures, coupled with growing scrutiny of corporate behavior by
  society and stakeholders, the environment is expected to become one in which compliance issues are more likely to arise.
    In this environment, we recognize violations of laws and regulations, deviations from social norms, and unethical conduct or
  behavior in the course of business activities as significant management risks, as they may not only damage our social reputation and
  the trust of our stakeholders but also have a serious impact on the continuity of our business and our corporate value.
    Specifically, we identify potential compliance risks throughout the entire business process, establish a framework that enables each
  organization to proactively address them, and continuously work to foster a sound and transparent corporate culture.
     However, if any compliance issues arise due to changes in the internal or external environment, or delays in the early identification
  of signs of misconduct or other improper conduct, the following risks may arise.
  - Decline in reputation
  - Loss of trust from stakeholders
  - Worsened business results and financial condition


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Establishment, promotion, and dissemination of the SHIONOGI Group Code of Conduct
  - Enhancing compliance awareness of all Group employees through Global Compliance & Quality Week
  - Operation of the compliance promotion system according to the structure of organizations
  - Meetings of the Compliance Committee chaired by the Representative Director, President and CEO (four times a year)
  - Reporting to the Board of Directors on the status of the Compliance Committee activities (twice a year)
  - Conducting compliance awareness surveys among all employees, and providing feedback on analysis results for each organization
  - Providing training to all employees globally on the checklist of five items for employees to stop and reassess their actions when they
    are unsure of what to do
  - Conducting rank-based training for Corporate Officers, Senior Vice Presidents and SHIONOGI Group company presidents,
   organization heads, etc.


(5) Environment and safety
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI recognizes that, in the course of its global business activities, including the research, development, manufacturing, and
  supply of pharmaceuticals, its use of facilities and equipment, as well as its consumption of raw materials and energy, may affect the
  environment and ecosystems. Furthermore, the progression of climate change and the increasing severity of natural disasters in recent
  years have created risk factors that may directly affect business activities. Appropriately addressing these environmental challenges is
  a crucial prerequisite for maintaining stable business operations. In addition, we recognize that environmental considerations are also
  important from the perspectives of procurement and business transactions, as negative environmental impacts may arise throughout
  the entire value chain, including raw material procurement and business partners. We recognize environmental risks as significant
  management risks, as environmental incidents and regulatory violations within our value chain, including those involving our own
  operations, as well as delays in implementing measures to reduce environmental impact, could affect the SHIONOGI Group’s business
  performance and corporate value through operational disruptions, additional response costs, expanded legal liability, and a decline in
  social reputation. Against this backdrop, under our EHS governance framework, we have established the “SHIONOGI Group EHS
  Action Targets” as our medium- to long-term targets and are promoting activities to reduce environmental impact across the entire
  value chain, with a focus on “climate change,” “resource conservation and circulation,” “water,” and “AMR.” In addition, we have
  established the “SHIONOGI Group Procurement Policy” and “SHIONOGI Group Business Partner Code of Conduct,” and we are
  continuously working to reduce environmental risks through collaborative engagement with our suppliers.




                                                                ―29―
    However, if we fail to adequately address environmental incidents, disputes with stakeholders, stricter regulations, or changes in
  social expectations, the following risks may arise.
  - Suspension of operation of facilities or equipment due to serious environmental incidents, human rights violations, legal violations,
    etc.
  - Incurrence or increase of countermeasures or recovery/repair costs
  - Lawsuits filed for compensation, or payment of compensation costs
  - Decline in profitability due to regulatory compliance and increased additional costs
  - Negative impact on corporate value resulting from a decline in social standing and reputation


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Establishment and implementation of the SHIONOGI Group policies to enhance environmental and human rights governance
  - Establishment of a Group-wide environment, health and safety (EHS) management system
  - Promotion of Medium-Term Action Plan regarding environmental materiality and EHS
  - Strengthening the operation of ISO 14001 and ISO 45001 and the EHS management system in accordance with these standards at
    each operating site
  - Ensuring compliance with relevant laws and regulations and promoting environmental initiatives by formulating stricter voluntary
    management standards and targets
  - Conducting environmental risk assessments of business partners and requesting improvements through engagement and audits


(6) Human rights
  <Overview>
    The source of SHIONOGI’s value creation is the people who work for the SHIONOGI Group. We believe that creating an
  environment where diverse personalities and values are respected and where people can mutually inspire and challenge one another
  leads to innovation and sustainable growth. Based on this understanding, addressing human rights issues, such as establishing fair
  labor practices, promoting equal opportunities, and creating a safe and fair workplace environment, will help secure the human
  resources needed to support corporate growth.
     In addition, as we conduct our business activities, including the research, development, manufacturing, and supply of
  pharmaceuticals, on a global scale, there is a possibility that human rights may be affected throughout the entire value chain, including
  our business partners and local communities. We recognize that effectively managing these impacts is essential for business continuity
  and growth.
    With the globalization of business and the increasing complexity of supply chains in recent years, the impact of a human rights
  incident may become more widespread. In addition, as our business areas expand through various business investments, the likelihood
  of new human rights risks emerging is also increasing. With respect to employees, incidents such as harassment, discrimination,
  excessive working hours, and inadequate attention to workplace safety may lead to reduced employee engagement and the loss of
  talent, thereby posing a risk to business operations and growth. Therefore, SHIONOGI recognizes the increasing importance of respect
  for human rights throughout the entire value chain, including among employees, business partners, and contractors.
    Specifically, there is a growing need to address a wide range of human rights issues, including the elimination of forced labor and
  child labor, the prevention of discrimination, fair treatment that respects diversity, the provision of a safe and healthy working
  environment, and consideration for local communities. Failure to adequately address these issues may adversely affect business
  continuity and corporate value through violations of laws and regulations and a decline in public trust.
    Based on this recognition, we have established the “SHIONOGI Group Human Rights Policy” to promote respect for the human
  rights of all people who may be affected by our business activities. We have also established the “SHIONOGI Group Procurement
  Policy” and the “SHIONOGI Group Business Partner Code of Conduct” and are working to reduce the risk of human rights violations
  through ongoing engagement with our business partners.




                                                                ―30―
    However, if we fail to adequately address incidents involving human rights violations, resulting disputes with stakeholders, stricter
  regulations, or changes in social expectations, the following risks may arise.
  - Restrictions or suspension of operations due to human rights violations, legal violations, etc.
  - Emergence of legal risks, such as the filing of lawsuits seeking damages
  - Reduced business opportunities due to requests for corrective action from customers and business partners, less favorable trading
    conditions, and exclusion from supplier selection
  - Decline in employee engagement, loss of talent, and difficulty in securing talent resulting from violations of fair labor practices
  - Decline in innovation capacity and medium- to long-term competitiveness due to hindered talent development and employees being
    prevented from realizing their full potential
  - Negative impact on corporate value resulting from a decline in social standing and reputation


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Establishment and implementation of the SHIONOGI Group Human Rights Policy to enhance human rights governance
  - Establishment of fair and appropriate evaluation and compensation systems
  - Implementation of a human resources system based on the principle of equal pay for equal work
  - Provision of a living wage that exceeds the minimum wage set in each region
  - Support for an environment that enables diverse work styles, such as flexible work hours and telecommuting
  - Creation of a safe and healthy work environment
  - Implementation of human rights risk assessments across the entire value chain, including suppliers
  - Regular engagement with suppliers, audits, and requests for improvement


(7) Partnerships with other companies
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI regards partnerships with other companies as one of its key management strategies for maximizing business value.
    In the field of infectious diseases, including HIV and AMR, SHIONOGI drives value creation that would be difficult to achieve on
  its own across research and development, manufacturing, sales, and efforts to expand access through collaboration with pharmaceutical
  companies, biotech ventures, national governments, and international organizations. Furthermore, in the QOL disease area, with
  acquisition of the edaravone business and advances in our rare disease pipeline, SHIONOGI is strengthening its R&D capabilities and
  business development capabilities through collaboration with external partners with expertise and technological capabilities.
     In recent years, with the expansion of business areas and increased M&A activity, the types of partners and the nature of our
  relationships with them have become even more diverse. In addition to joint research and licensing agreements, the forms of
  collaboration, including business partnerships, alliances, and collaborations with governments and public institutions, have also
  become more complex. We recognize that effectively managing these partnerships and maximizing value by leveraging their respective
  strengths are essential to achieving global growth in both the infectious disease and QOL disease areas.
     However, the following risks may arise due to misunderstanding regarding the division of roles and responsibilities with partners,
  inadequate information management, and changes in collaborative relationships.
  - Decline in business results and synergies
  - Leakage of SHIONOGI’s technologies, know-how, and confidential information to external parties
  - Lawsuits due to unintended, unauthorized use of technologies of other companies or infringement of intellectual property by
    SHIONOGI
  - Decline in the brand image, reputation, or trust from investors due to leakage of confidential information by other companies


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Clarification of strategies and division of roles, and maintenance and strengthening of trust through communication with partners
  - Clarification of conditions regarding intellectual property rights, information management, and damages in non-disclosure
    agreements, licensing agreements, etc.
  - Conducting due diligence for in-licensing, out-licensing, M&A, and alliances, including intellectual property, legal, and financial
    aspects
  - Strengthening data and information management systems through minimizing information and access control
  - Conducting periodic evaluations and audits of partner companies’ governance, financial status, compliance systems, etc.
  - Establishing a monitoring system across all partnerships to enable the early identification of issues and risks




                                                                 ―31―
(8) Intellectual property
  <Overview>
    SHIONOGI’s products and R&D outcomes are protected by intellectual property rights (e.g., patents) and intangible assets such as
  technology and know-how, thereby ensuring SHIONOGI’s competitive advantage and contributing to future revenue generation.
     With the acquisitions of Torii Pharmaceutical, the JT Pharmaceutical Business, and the edaravone business, SHIONOGI’s business
  areas and R&D approaches have been increasingly diverse. This may make it more difficult to adequately protect our intellectual
  property in the future and may also increase the likelihood of infringing the intellectual property rights of third parties. Furthermore,
  in recent years, intellectual property rights and contractual arrangements have become increasingly complex as open innovation has
  advanced through business acquisitions, joint research, and licensing transactions and competition in the global market has intensified.
  Consequently, the risk of disputes and litigation over the ownership and validity of intellectual property rights is increasing, and the
  likelihood of unexpected intellectual property infringements is also increasing. Based on this recognition, we position intellectual
  property as a form of technological capital that supports medium- to long-term value creation and are working to ensure its proper
  protection and utilization while reducing the risk of infringement.
     However, if the intellectual property rights SHIONOGI owns are infringed by a third party, or SHIONOGI’s products infringe the
  intellectual property rights of a third party, the possible risks are as follows.
  - Decline in competitive advantage and a decrease in expected revenue resulting from loss of intellectual property protection
  - Occurrence of disputes and litigation regarding intellectual property rights, and increased costs associated with responding to them
  - Payment of damages
  - Injunction against manufacture and sale of the product, or restrictions on business activities
  - Decline in the corporate brand or reputation resulting from the above


  <Major responses and initiatives>
  - Proper acquisition of intellectual property rights and establishment of a management system, and continuous surveillance on
    infringement of rights by third parties
  - Conducting infringement prevention surveys in business activities
  - Putting in place a system to prevent infringement by carrying out IP due diligence in in-licensing and out-licensing and alliance
    activities
  - Enhancing business and R&D strategies through the analysis and utilization of intellectual property information using IP landscapes
  - Continuously conducting employee training on intellectual property and intangible assets through e-learning, etc.


(9) Systems and governments
  <Overview>
    The Pharmaceuticals Business is subject to a range of regulations due to various government policies in each country and region.
  In addition to tighter health insurance finances, the U.S. Inflation Reduction Act (IRA), the most favored nation policy (MFN), and
  other laws may further increase the pressure to curb drug cost increases, especially in developed countries. We also need to pay close
  attention to the impact of the changes in government in the U.S. and other countries around the world on their policies and international
  relations. In Japan, government policy trends, such as the health insurance system reforms in anticipation of an increase in healthcare
  expenses due to the further aging of the population and the annual NHI price revisions, may affect SHIONOGI’s performance. In
  addition, changes in regulations in Japan and overseas related to the development and manufacture of pharmaceuticals, as well as
  legislation requiring the disclosure of information related to business activities and social responsibility, may result in additional costs
  or the need for new measures. If any of such events emerges, the possible risks are as follows.
  - Decline in the predictability of the prescription drug business
  - Determination of drug prices deviating from the created innovation value
  - Delay in research and development, or supply instability of drugs, vaccines, etc.
  - Decrease in sales and profits of drugs, vaccines, etc.
  - Decline in reputation or investor confidence




                                                                  ―32―
<Major responses and initiatives>
- Creating innovative pharmaceuticals and healthcare services and providing them at prices acceptable to society
- Building evidence that demonstrates the value of its innovations
- Promoting efforts to appeal to the value of innovations through the activities of industry associations
- Obtaining the latest information on the NHI drug price system and various regulations on the research and development, manufacture,
  and sale of pharmaceuticals, etc., and promptly responding to them
- Promoting initiatives to ensure accountability and strengthening information disclosure


  Beyond the above significant risks, there are various other risks that may affect SHIONOGI’s business performance and financial
condition, such as those related to litigation, pandemics and natural disasters, and financial markets and foreign exchange trends.
Those listed here do not represent all the risks for SHIONOGI.




                                                             ―33―
4. Analysis of Financial Position, Operating Results, and Cash Flows by Management
   The following is a summary of the SHIONOGI Group’s (the Company and its consolidated subsidiaries) awareness, analysis, and
discussion on its operating results, etc. from the management’s perspective.
  In addition, SHIONOGI has a single business segment, consisting of the research & development, purchase, manufacture, and sale of
prescription drugs and related businesses.
  Matters discussed here that are not historical fact reflect the judgment of SHIONOGI as of the end of the fiscal year under review.


(1) Awareness, analysis, and discussion on operating results, etc. for the fiscal year under review
  i. Operating Results, etc.
  a. Financial Position
     As of March 31, 2026, total assets were 2,576,870 million yen, an increase of 1,041,521 million yen from the end of the previous
  fiscal year.
     Non-current assets increased, primarily due to an increase in investments accounted for using the equity method, following an
  additional investment in ViiV, which resulted in the company becoming an equity-method affiliate. Furthermore, due to increases in
  property, plant and equipment, goodwill, and intangible assets following the consolidation of Torii Pharmaceutical as a subsidiary
  and the absorption-type split of the JT pharmaceutical business, non-current assets totaled 1,266,535 million yen, an increase of
  589,690 million yen from the end of the previous fiscal year. Please note that the amounts of goodwill, intangible assets, etc. are
  provisionally calculated amounts as allocation of the acquisition cost has not yet been completed. Current assets were 1,310,335
  million yen, an increase of 451,831 million yen from the end of the previous fiscal year, due to increases in cash and cash equivalents
  resulting from borrowings associated with large investments, fixed-term deposits of more than three months (included in other
  financial assets under current assets), and trade receivables.
     Equity totaled 1,686,205 million yen, an increase of 323,708 million yen from the end of the previous fiscal year, due to recording
  of profits and other factors despite a decrease due to dividend payments and other components of equity.
     Liabilities totaled 890,665 million yen, an increase of 717,813 million yen from the end of the previous fiscal year.
     Non-current liabilities were 63,235 million yen, an increase of 19,775 million yen from the end of the previous fiscal year, mainly
  due to an increase in deferred tax liabilities. Current liabilities were 827,430 million yen, an increase of 698,037 million yen from
  the end of the previous fiscal year, mainly due to an increase in borrowings associated with large investments.
  b. Operating Results
  For the year ended March 31, 2026 (April 1, 2025 to March 31, 2026), operating results were as follows.

     Revenue was 499.7 billion yen (up 14.0 percent year on year). In addition to the steady growth of overseas business and royalty
  income, our domestic business expanded significantly following the acquisition of Torii Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd. as a consolidated
  subsidiary through M&A of the Japan Tobacco Inc. (hereinafter, “JT”) Group’s pharmaceutical business (JT pharmaceutical business,
  domestic group company Torii Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd., and U.S. group company Akros), and the recording of its revenue. As a
  result, each business made steady progress, and revenue for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 exceeded that of the previous fiscal
  year, marking a record high for the fourth consecutive year.




                                                                 ―34―
  In terms of profits, despite a year-on-year increase in expenses, mainly selling, general and administrative expenses, as a result of
actively making preparations for the launch of new products in the U.S., and post-merger integration (PMI) activities related to
business investments for future growth, operating profit increased 6.5 percent to 166.7 billion yen, marking a record high for the
fourth consecutive year. Profit before tax was 238.9 billion yen, a 19.0 percent increase year on year, and profit attributable to owners
of parent was 205.2 billion yen, a 20.4 percent increase year on year, and EBITDA increased 4.7 percent year on year to 187.7 billion
yen.
  In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, we achieved record-high results in revenue and for all profit items, while making
significant business investments aimed at strengthening our management and business foundations to realize our 2030 Vision.


・ Domestic sales of prescription drugs
 Domestic sales of prescription drugs increased 25.0 percent year on year to 123.5 billion yen. Since Torii Pharmaceutical became a
consolidated subsidiary on September 1, 2025, its sales for seven months have been recorded. In addition, by co-promoting key
products of Shionogi & Co., Ltd. and Torii Pharmaceutical, we were able to leverage each company’s strengths to provide information
to medical institutions in a mutually complementary manner, thereby achieving an increase in revenue.
   By product category, revenue from acute respiratory infection drugs was 33.8 billion yen, a 34.8 percent decrease year on year.
Sales of the anti-coronavirus drug Xocova decreased due to the low prevalence of COVID-19 compared to the previous fiscal year.
On the other hand, sales of the anti-influenza virus drug Xofluza increased in line with the spread of influenza during the winter.
Although both drugs maintained a high market share in their respective treatment drug markets and generated a certain level of revenue,
overall revenue in the acute respiratory infections field fell short of the level of the previous fiscal year. In the QOL disease field,
prescriptions of QUVIVIQ, an insomnia treatment, have increased as one year has passed since its launch and the 14-day prescription
restriction has been lifted. As a result, revenue increased 224.1 percent to 2.6 billion yen, significantly exceeding that of the previous
fiscal year. Furthermore, in March 2026, sales commenced for ZURZUVAE, a treatment drug for depression with a novel mechanism
of action.


・ Overseas subsidiary sales and exports
  Revenue from overseas business increased 9.9 percent to 65.0 billion yen from the previous year. Due to strong sales of Cefiderocol
(product name in U.S.: Fetroja, product name in Europe: Fetcroja), revenues from businesses in the U.S. and Europe were 28.7 billion
yen, a 22.9 percent increase, and 20.8 billion yen, a 23.4 percent increase, respectively. Revenue from our China business was 6.2
billion yen, a 28.3 percent decrease. This is mainly due to a decline in sales of generic drugs resulting from healthcare cost-containment
policies. Meanwhile, we made steady progress in transitioning to a new drug business. We obtained approval for Cefiderocol in January
2026 for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections caused by Gram-negative bacteria, and our new drug application for
Naldemedine, a treatment for opioid-induced constipation, was accepted in May 2025.


・Royalty income and dividend income from ViiV
 Royalty income from ViiV Healthcare Ltd. (hereinafter, “ViiV”) increased 8.7 percent from the previous year to 261.3 billion yen
due to the strong growth of oral two-drug combinations and long-acting formulations (LA formulations). Other royalty income
increased significantly to 17.3 billion yen, up 304.9 percent year on year, supported by strong sales of the anti-influenza drug Xofluza,
which is out-licensed to Roche (Switzerland), as well as the recognition of new royalties related to the JT pharmaceutical business,
which the Company acquired on December 1, 2025. Dividend income from ViiV increased 30.0 percent to 52.4 billion yen due to the
steady progress of ViiV’s business.




                                                               ―35―
  ・ Objective indicators for determining the achievement status of management goals
    As described in “II. Business Overview, 1. Management Policy, Business Environment, Issues to Be Addressed, etc.,” SHIONOGI
  revised STS2030 and formulated the STS2030 Revision in June 2023.
     In the STS2030 Revision, three indicators for growth potential and three indicators for shareholder return have been set as the
  financial and management indicators to be achieved. The three indicators we set to measure growth potential are as follows: revenue,
  as we prioritize top-line growth; overseas sales CAGR (Compound Annual Growth Rate), as we aim to achieve growth globally; and
  EBITDA (Earnings Before Interest, Taxes, Depreciation, and Amortization), as we make active investments toward growth and to
  measure our earning power. As the three indicators for shareholder return, we continued to set EPS, DOE, and ROE from the
  perspectives of business growth and financial measures.
    In FY2025, the Company made several large-scale business investments as it entered a new phase of growth. Accordingly, the
  Company plans to revise its management indicators in its forthcoming medium-term business plan. Going forward, we will steadily
  implement each of the measures we have identified, continue to explore business development opportunities, including M&A, in-
  licensing, and partnerships, to establish additional revenue drivers, and leverage our strong financial foundation to make proactive
  investments commensurate with the value of each project, with the aim of achieving our management indicators.

    Net cash used in investing activities was 506,137 million yen, an increase of 390,056 million yen year on year, mainly due to

     Net cash provided in financing activities was 599,321 million yen, an increase of 664,229 million yen year on year, due to income

                                                                         ―40―
 (4) Agreements on governance between companies and shareholders
        In establishing the SHIONOGI Infectious Disease Research Promotion Foundation (“the Foundation”), the Company established
     a trust for the benefit of a third party (“the Trust”), with Sumitomo Mitsui Trust Bank, Limited as the trustee, Custody Bank of
     Japan, Ltd. as the sub-trustee, and the Foundation as the beneficiary. The Trust has acquired shares in the Company. Under the trust
     agreement, it has been agreed that the voting rights attached to the Company’s shares held under the Trust will not be exercised
     throughout the term of the Trust.

 (i) Outline of the agreement

Date of the agreement                Counterparty                   Address of the counterparty                   Description of the agreement

                                                                                                       Establishment of the trust and restrictions on the
September 1, 2022        Sumitomo Mitsui Trust Bank, Limited   1-4-1 Marunouchi, Chiyoda-ku, Tokyo
                                                                                                       exercise of voting rights attached to the shares


 (ii) Purpose of the agreement
    The purpose of establishing the Trust is to enable the Foundation to carry out social contribution activities on a continuous and
 stable basis in line with its founding objectives. In light of the above purpose, restrictions have been placed on the exercise of voting
 rights attached to the Company’s shares held under the Trust.


 (iii) Board deliberations and the Company’s decision-making process relating to the agreement
    At the Board of Directors meeting held on May 11, 2022, the establishment of the Foundation and the creation of the Trust were
 resolved.


 (iv) Impact of the agreement on our corporate governance
    The Trust currently holds 9 million shares of the Company, and we recognize that the impact on our corporate governance is limited.

                                                                     ―43―
6. Research and Development Activities
  During the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, we actively advanced our research and development activities, focusing on projects in
our priority areas of infectious diseases and QOL diseases with significant social impact.

  (1) Research
  Research on S-567123, a vaccine designed to prevent the onset of infection by inducing antibodies that suppress the activity of
sarbecoviruses (a group of viruses including SARS coronavirus [SARS-CoV-1] and novel coronavirus [SARS-CoV-2]), has progressed,
and a Phase 1 clinical trial has begun. This vaccine is a next-generation vaccine expected to be effective against emerging variants of
SARS-CoV-2, as it induces antibodies that target regions of the virus where mutations are unlikely to occur.
  Furthermore, research on S-898270, a therapeutic candidate for improving symptoms of Alzheimer’s disease, has progressed, and a
Phase 1 clinical trial has been initiated. This drug is expected to improve cognitive function by enhancing neuronal and synaptic functions.
  In addition to advancing these research activities, we completed the acquisition of the JT Group’s pharmaceutical business with the
aim of strengthening our in-house drug discovery capabilities, particularly in small-molecule drug discovery. Through this acquisition,
we enhanced our drug discovery infrastructure by expanding our talent base of experienced medicinal chemists and other specialists,
and by acquiring and integrating advanced technology platforms, such as AI and quantum computing, from the JT pharmaceutical
business. We aim to further enhance our strengths as a drug discovery-based pharmaceutical company and generate globally competitive
in-house developed products.


  (2) Development
  In the field of infectious diseases, based on the positive results of the global Phase 3 post-exposure prophylaxis study (SCORPIO-
PEP) for ensitrelvir (product name in Japan: Xocova), an oral antiviral drug for COVID-19, we submitted an application for approval in
the United States for the prevention of COVID-19, and the application was accepted by the U.S. FDA. Subsequently, on May 29, 2026,
we received approval in the U.S. for the prevention of COVID-19. In Europe, we submitted marketing authorization applications for
both post-exposure prophylaxis and treatment indications. In addition, in Japan, we obtained approval for post-exposure prophylaxis and
also submitted a manufacturing and marketing authorization application for treatment in children aged 6 to 11 years. Furthermore, we
initiated a Phase 3 clinical trial to expand the age range to include children aged 0 to 5 years.
  Regarding S-892216, a next-generation antiviral drug for COVID-19, we obtained positive results in a Phase 2 clinical trial toward
obtaining approval as an oral treatment and advanced preparations for a Phase 3 clinical trial. Furthermore, development of long-acting
formulations has progressed toward obtaining approval for pre-exposure prophylaxis for the purpose of preventing infection.
  Regarding S-268024, a COVID-19 vaccine targeting the JN.1 lineage of the SARS-CoV-2 variant, the primary endpoint was achieved
in a Phase 3 clinical trial, demonstrating efficacy and a favorable safety profile. Based on these results, we submitted an application for
partial change to the manufacturing and marketing approval in Japan.
  Regarding baloxavir marboxil (product name in Japan: Xofluza), an anti-influenza drug, we obtained manufacturing and marketing
approval in Japan for a granule formulation intended for pediatric patients. This has made it possible to offer treatment options to a wider
range of patients.
  Cefiderocol, an injectable antibacterial agent for the treatment of Gram-negative bacterial infections, including those caused by
multidrug-resistant bacteria, was approved in China for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections.
 In the field of QOL diseases with significant social impact, we initiated a global Phase 2 clinical trial of S-606001, a small-molecule
oral treatment candidate for the rare disease Pompe disease. This disease is a genetic metabolic disorder characterized by muscle
weakness and respiratory dysfunction, with significant unmet medical needs that existing treatments have not fully addressed, and this
drug is expected to provide a new treatment option.
  Regarding zuranolone (product name in Japan: ZURZUVAE), an oral antidepressant with a novel mechanism of action, we obtained
manufacturing and marketing approval in Japan. This drug is effective when taken orally once a day for 14 days. In a Phase 3 clinical
trial in Japan, it demonstrated a significant improvement in depressive symptoms compared with placebo as early as two days after the
start of administration, suggesting a rapid onset of action.
   Regarding S-531011, an anti-CCR8 antibody for solid tumors, we obtained results from the Phase 1 portion of a Phase 1/2 clinical
trial and initiated a Phase 2 clinical trial.
   Furthermore, following the M&A of the JT Group’s pharmaceutical business, we acquired several promising development products
from the former JT pharmaceutical business and Torii Pharmaceutical.


  As a result of these activities, research and development expenses for the entire Group for the fiscal year under review totaled 122,843
million yen.

III. Equipment and Facilities
1. Capital Expenditures
  SHIONOGI (the Company and its consolidated subsidiaries) makes continuous capital expenditures in production, research, and sales
to facilitate activities such as sales expansion, cost reduction, new product launches, and research and development.
   In the fiscal year under review, capital expenditures totaled ¥18,268 million, up ¥5,983 million (48.7%) from the previous fiscal year,
primarily spent on research facilities and production facilities.
   The required funds for these expenditures were mainly allocated from cash on hand and subsidies.
  There have been no sales, removals, or losses of fixed assets that would have a material impact on production capacity.

3. Plans for Additions and Disposals of Facilities
  Capital expenditure by SHIONOGI (the Company and its consolidated subsidiaries) is planned based on a comprehensive
consideration of the effects of the expenditure, taking into account factors such as future demand forecasts, the status of new product
development, and the need to upgrade existing facilities. In principle, each company formulates its own separate capital plan, but the
filing company plays a central role in coordinating these plans to avoid duplication of expenditures across SHIONOGI.
   As of the end of the fiscal year under review, the planned expenditure in the new construction, expansion, etc. of principal facilities
was ¥37,005 million. Of this, ¥5,770 million has already been paid, and the remaining ¥31,235 million is to be funded mainly by cash
on hand and subsidies.
  Plans for principal facilities that are currently under way are for the expansion of research, manufacturing, and other facilities in
SHIONOGI, and are as follows.

*1. The persons to be allotted share acquisition rights, the number of these persons, and the number of share acquisition rights allotted
    to these persons are as follows.
        Company directors (excluding directors who are Audit and Supervisory Committee Members and outside directors; the same
        applies hereinafter) 3 persons, 252 rights
        Company executive officers (excluding those concurrently serving as directors; the same applies hereinafter) 9 persons, 270 rights
    The number of shares per share acquisition right (hereinafter referred to as the “Number of Shares Granted”) shall be 300.
*2. In the event that, after the date of allotment of share acquisition rights (hereinafter referred to as the “Allotment Date”), the Company
    conducts a stock split (including gratis allotment of shares of common stock of the Company; the same shall apply hereinafter with
    respect to stock splits) or a reverse stock split, the Number of Shares Granted shall be adjusted in accordance with the following
    calculation for those share acquisition rights that have not been exercised at the time of such stock split or reverse stock split.
    Adjusted number of shares = Number of shares before adjustment* Split or consolidation ratio
    In addition to the above, in the event that the Company conducts a merger, corporate split or share exchange after the Allotment Date,
    or otherwise if the Number of Shares Granted requires adjustment, the Company’s Board of Directors may adjust the Number of
    Shares Granted as it deems necessary.
    Any fraction of less than one share resulting from the above adjustment shall be truncated.
*3. i. The issue price is the sum of the fair value of the share acquisition rights on the Allotment Date and the amount to be paid upon
       exercise of the share acquisition rights (1 yen per share).
       In addition, the recipient of share acquisition right allotment (hereinafter referred to as “Share Acquisition Rights Holder”) may, in
       lieu of payment of the amount equivalent to the fair value of the share acquisition rights, offset this with compensation claims
       against the Company.
   ii. All shares to be issued to Share Acquisition Rights Holders upon exercise of share acquisition rights are to be issued from treasury
       shares, and no new shares will be issued as a result of the exercise of share acquisition rights.
       In the event that treasury shares are used, no amount shall be paid into capital.
*4. i. During the exercise period for share acquisition rights, directors who are Share Acquisition Rights Holders may only exercise all
       of their share acquisition rights in a single act, solely during the period from the day following the day on which they lose their
       position as a director of the Company until the day on which 10 days (if the 10th day falls on a holiday, the next business day) have
       passed.
   ii. During the exercise period for share acquisition rights, executive officers who are Share Acquisition Rights Holders may only
       exercise all of their share acquisition rights in a single act, solely from the day following the day on which they retire as executive
       officer of the Company or the day their employment contract with the Company is terminated (excluding employment contracts
       related to re-employment after mandatory retirement), whichever is later, until the day on which 10 days (if the 10th day falls on a
       holiday, the next business day) have passed.
       In the event that an executive officer who is a Share Acquisition Rights Holder is newly appointed as a director of the Company,
       they may not exercise these rights until their retirement as a director.
  iii. In the event of the death of a Share Acquisition Rights Holder, their heirs may exercise their share acquisition rights only during
       the period from the day following the day of the death of the benefactor and the day after 6 months have passed.
  iv. Other conditions for exercising rights shall be as set forth in the “Share Acquisition Rights Allotment Agreement” concluded
       between the Company and the Share Acquisition Rights Holders.
*5. In the event that the Company conducts a merger (limited to cases in which the Company is dissolved due to merger), absorption-
    type split or incorporation-type split (limited to cases in which the Company becomes a split company), or share exchange or share
    transfer (limited to cases where the Company becomes a wholly-owned subsidiary) (the above are hereinafter collectively referred
    to as “Reorganization”), Share Acquisition Rights Holders of the remaining share acquisition rights as of the time immediately
    preceding the effective date of the Reorganization (hereinafter referred to as “Residual Share Acquisition Rights”) shall, in each
    circumstance, be issued the share acquisition rights of the stock company as prescribed in each of Article 236, Paragraph 1, Item 8,
    (a) through (e) of the Companies Act (hereinafter referred to as the “Reorganized Company”) in accordance with the terms and
    conditions of the issuance of these share acquisition rights. However, this shall be limited to cases where it is stipulated in the
    absorption-type merger agreement, incorporation-type merger agreement, absorption-type split agreement, incorporation-type split
    plan, share exchange agreement, or share transfer plan that share acquisition rights of the Reorganized Company will be delivered in
    accordance with conditions equivalent to the terms and conditions of issuance of these share acquisition rights.

    on August 30, 2024, “Number of shares corresponding to share acquisition rights” and “Issue price and amount paid into capital on
    issuance of shares from exercise of share acquisition rights” have been adjusted.

3. Dividend Policy
  The SHIONOGI Group promotes measures that balance growth investment and shareholder returns, with the aim of maximizing
corporate value and allowing shareholders to experience medium- to long-term profit growth. Regarding dividends, SHIONOGI set a
target of 4.0% or more in dividends on equity, which it aims to increase steadily in accordance with corporate value growth.
   For the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, SHIONOGI elected to pay a year-end dividend of 38 yen per share , an increase of 5 yen
from the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025. As a result, the total annual dividend, including the interim dividend, will be 71 yen per share.
  With regard to retained earnings, SHIONOGI will make active investments both in Japan and overseas to “expand areas by leveraging
our strengths in infectious diseases” and “establish revenue drivers” in order to transform itself into a Healthcare as a Service (HaaS)
company that provides healthcare services.
  The Articles of Incorporation stipulate that the Company may pay dividends from surplus twice a year, an interim dividend and a year-
end dividend. The decision-making bodies for these dividends from surplus are the General Meeting of Shareholders for the year-end
dividend and the Board of Directors for the interim dividend.


(Note) Dividends from surplus for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 are as follows. The total dividend amount of 32,679 million yen
and the dividend per share of 38 yen for the year-end dividend are included in the matters for resolution of the Annual General Meeting
of Shareholders scheduled to be held on June 24, 2026.
